Can Portugal Retire Cristiano Ronaldo’s No.7 Shirt

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Many wondered if this news would ever come, but yes, Cristiano Ronaldo has finally retired from international football. Now 41, the veteran forward has played his final game for Portugal.

The Al-Nassr star bows out with 146 goals in 234 caps for his country, having also won Euro 2016. In his departing message, he said: "To the Portuguese people, the day has come to return the number 7 jersey of our national team. I never felt it was exclusively mine. But for 23 years I had the privilege and responsibility of wearing it, defending it, and taking it with me to stadiums all over the world. Today the moment has come to hand it over to the future. It wasn't an easy decision, because there are decisions that aren't made only with the head. They are also made with the heart."

For club and country, the number seven shirt has become synonymous with Ronaldo. As such, despite his claims that it's 'time to return the jersey,' some are wondering if Portugal will instead retire the number as an acknowledgement of his special career.

However, it might not be quite so simple as just making one decision.

The 41-year-old has retired from international football, havign scored a record 146 goals in 234 Portugal caps.

A little-known FIFA rule stops international shirt numbers from being retired. Ronaldo actually started out wearing No.16 when he made his debut in 2003, but was handed the No.7 shirt in 2008. He wore it as he lifted Euro 2016 and two Nations League titles, while also becoming the all-time men’s international top goalscorer.

Had he achieved that level of success at a club team, maybe he would have seen the shirt number retired. Johan Cruyff (14 at Ajax), Paolo Maldini (3 at AC Milan), and Franco Baresi (6 also at Milan) are all among the legendary footballers to have had their shirt numbers retired, while Liverpool paid respect to Diogo Jota by retiring his No.20 jersey after his tragic passing in 2025.

Ronaldo, though, will have to pass on the number seven shirt to another Portuguese star. This is because national teams are required to designate consecutive numbers for every player in the squad for FIFA and FIFA confederation tournaments. At the 2026 World Cup, for example, sides were made up of 26 players, meaning squad numbers had to go from 1 to 26. As such, it would be a breach of rules to skip past the number seven on a tournament-going squad list.

Perhaps this isn't the worst rule in the world. After all, Argentina once tried to retire the No.10 shirt in honour of World Cup-winning great Diego Maradona. They had a request denied by FIFA ahead of the 2002 World Cup, but the number was fittingly later won by Lionel Messi, who would win the 2022 World Cup and match his predecessor's legacy.

Despite also having many past greats, Portugal have never requested to retire a number before. Ballon d'Or winner Luis Figo used to wear No.7, while the likes of Nani and Ricardo Quaresma even donned it while Ronaldo was absent.

It's worth noting that when Ronaldo quit the Portugal camp early in September, Rafael Leao took on the vacant No.7 shirt for the two remaining Nations League fixtures. It remains to be seen if he will wear that number in the upcoming break later this month.

Cloudflare shares surge as revenue growth accelerates to 39% and outlook rises

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Shares in Cloudflare Inc. jumped more than 14% in late trading today after the connectivity cloud company smashed third-quarter expectations and lifted its full-year outlook well above what Wall Street had been modeling. The gain more than wiped out an 8% fall in the stock during the regular session.

For the quarter ended on Sept. 30, Cloudflare reported adjusted earnings per share of 38 cents, up from 27 cents in the same quarter of 2025, on revenue of $782.5 million, up 39% year-over-year. Analysts were expecting earnings of 34 cents per share on revenue of $736.8 million.

Revenue growth hit its fastest pace in nearly four years as the annualized run rate crossed $3 billion. Current remaining performance obligations rose 37% from a year earlier. The adjusted operating margin also improved, to 17.5% from 15.3%, after shrinking year-over-year in each of the two prior quarters.

Because stock-based compensation ran to $174.1 million, Cloudflare still lost $34.1 million on an unadjusted basis, compared with a $1.3 million loss a year earlier. Another $11 million went to restructuring from the roughly 1,100 job cuts announced in May. Free cash flow slipped to 10% of revenue from 13% a year ago.

On the product side, short-lived blocking rules that write themselves during a live attack are the core of Adaptive Intelligence, which Cloudflare added to its Bot Management product in late August. AI agents got tools of their own earlier in the month through Cloudflare Wallets and cloudflare.pay, which give an agent an identity handle and will let it pay for services from a stablecoin balance. Federal agencies can now put more sensitive workloads on Cloudflare for Government, which reached FedRAMP High status in August.

Co-founder and Chief Executive Matthew Prince called the period “Cloudflare’s best quarter ever” in the earnings release. Cloudflare has built the development platform for the AI-powered internet, and organizations of every size keep turning to it for protection as digital threats escalate, he said. Prince added that the business has “found an even higher gear for this next phase of growth.”

Cloudflare also named Christian Smith president of revenue today, with responsibility for worldwide sales and revenue operations. He replaces Mark Anderson, who had already said he would retire from the full-time role at year-end. Smith previously ran sales at Splunk Inc. as chief revenue officer.

For the fourth quarter, Cloudflare expects adjusted earnings of 43 cents per share on revenue of $845 million to $846 million. Analysts had forecast 38 cents per share on revenue of $798.2 million.

Cloudflare now expects 2026 revenue of $2.963 billion to $2.964 billion, about $97 million above the midpoint of the range it gave in August. Analysts had been looking for $2.87 billion in revenue and $1.26 in adjusted earnings per share for the year, and the company is now guiding to $1.35.

Treasury bought back $6B in bonds, but dealers say bigger isn’t enough

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Treasury's latest $6 billion buyback shows why Wall Street dealers want the government to rethink how it buys bonds, not just how much it buys.

The US Treasury bought back $6 billion of older government bonds on Nov. 4, hitting the full limit of its latest operation. On paper, that looks like a success for a program designed to make the Treasury market easier to trade.

But there's a catch. One bond accounted for $4.9 billion, or 82% of everything Treasury agreed to buy. Add another $950 million from a second bond, and just two securities made up almost 98% of the entire purchase. Of the 40 bonds eligible for the operation, only seven were bought.

This is the kind of concentration Wall Street dealers have been worried about for months. In feedback published by Treasury on Nov. 4, dealers said the larger buybacks were helping market liquidity.

However, they also explained why simply increasing the program size might not be enough to achieve that.

Their feedback came before Wednesday's operation, so they weren't responding to this particular result. We now know the Treasury can buy billions of dollars of bonds and still leave dealers holding the securities they hoped to sell. The amount purchased is important, but so is how those purchases are spread around.

The Treasury market is enormous, but not every government bond trades equally easily. The newest issues attract the most attention and typically have the deepest markets. Older bonds, known as off-the-run Treasuries, can be harder to sell quickly without accepting a lower price.

That's where dealers come in. They buy bonds from customers, keep them on their books, and look for someone else willing to take them.

But holding those bonds costs money, sometimes a lot of money. That's why dealers often have to borrow to finance their positions, exposing them to price changes while they wait for another buyer. The longer they hold a bond, the more those costs and risks compound.

Treasury's liquidity-support buybacks give them another potential buyer: the government itself.

In September, Treasury began larger buybacks of longer-dated bonds, at least doubling the previous $2 billion maximum for those operations. The idea was to provide a more reliable outlet for older securities.

According to the Nov. 4 dealer feedback, most dealers reported at least modest improvements. They said the purchases had made it easier to find an exit for older bonds, reduced the risk of sudden price distortions, and helped keep pricing relationships between similar securities more stable.

While that's meaningful progress, the Treasury got mixed answers when it asked whether the changes had improved dealers' ability to handle customer trades.

A lack of unity among dealers is what makes these buybacks so controversial. We now have dealers that welcome a large government buyer but aren't necessarily becoming more willing to buy bonds from customers.

If the Treasury keeps concentrating its purchases in a handful of issues, dealers holding other, older issues still have to find other buyers or keep financing the position. In other words, the buyback program can work without working equally well across the entire market.

Dealers wanted better, earlier communication about operations, smaller purchases more frequently, and limits that would help distribute Treasury buying across a wider range of individual securities.

More frequent operations would give dealers more opportunities to sell, as they'd get another chance sooner.

Limits on how much of one security the Treasury can buy could also prevent a single bond from absorbing most of the available capacity. Each bond has its own identifying number, called a CUSIP, so dealers suggested using per-CUSIP limits to spread purchases more broadly.

Some dealers specifically warned about the risk of missing a concentrated buyback and being left to manage the interest-rate exposure from bonds that remained on their books.

This is exactly what we saw on Wednesday. According to the official data, Treasury received $39.6 billion in offers for an operation capped at $6 billion. That means dealers and other eligible participants offered more than six times what Treasury ultimately accepted.

And despite that demand, one bond maturing in November 2041 absorbed more than 80% of the purchases.

Nov. 4, 2026 operation, accepted face value in USD. Labels identify coupon and maturity. All seven purchased securities are shown; 40 were eligible. Eligibility does not show which unpurchased issues were offered. Face value is not cash expenditure. The largest share is calculated as $4.924 billion / $6 billion. Settlement: Nov. 5.

Source: U.S. Treasury: Nov. 4 buyback result, pages 1–2

It's worth being careful about what those numbers tell us. Treasury doesn't publish the full breakdown of rejected offers, so we don't know whether every one of the 33 eligible bonds that received no purchases was actually offered. We also can't assume Treasury made a bad decision by concentrating its buying.

This is because buybacks are extremely competitive operations. Treasury evaluates the prices offered and the relative value of the securities, so if one bond presents particularly attractive offers, buying more of it may make sense for taxpayers.

The problem is that getting the best available prices and helping the widest range of sellers aren't necessarily the same thing.

Nov. 4, 2026 operation, USD face value. Treasury accepted $6 billion against $39.636 billion offered, filling the $6 billion maximum. These overlapping measures are compared, not added. The offer ratio is 39.636 / 6 = 6.606, rounded to 6.61. Offered face value does not count distinct sellers or unique inventory. Filling the maximum is not a measured liquidity improvement.

Source: U.S. Treasury: Nov. 4 buyback result, operation totals

Treasury also has existing limits designed to preserve the amount of individual securities circulating in the market. Dealers are asking for changes that could distribute purchases more evenly within those constraints.

They discussed other possibilities, including alternative pricing methods and transactions that would exchange one security for another. But those would be more complicated to introduce, and Treasury said they would require further work.

For now, the most immediate requests are simpler: communicate earlier, buy more often, and avoid having so much of the operation go to just one security.

For investors watching Treasury buybacks as a potential source of liquidity for Bitcoin, a $6 billion buyback doesn't automatically mean $6 billion in fresh liquidity is headed toward risk assets.

First, the $6 billion is the face value of the debt Treasury agreed to retire. Because those bonds were trading below face value, the cash purchase amount was approximately $4 billion before accrued interest.

Second, Treasury continues issuing new debt to finance the government even as it buys back older securities.

So the program primarily changes which bonds are outstanding and how easily dealers can manage them. It doesn't automatically increase the amount of money available to buy Bitcoin.

However, a connection may still exist through financial conditions. An Oct. 30 Federal Reserve study, using data through June 2025, found that Treasury buybacks reduced dealers' bond inventories and eased some risk constraints for trading desks.

But the researchers found no statistically significant reduction in dealers' repo activity.

Repo is essentially short-term borrowing backed by securities. It's one way dealers and their customers finance bond positions. That's important because taking bonds off dealers' books doesn't necessarily eliminate the financing demands tied to the broader market.

For Bitcoin to benefit, easier Treasury trading would need to translate into better financing conditions or greater investor capacity to take risk. Wednesday's purchase result alone can't tell us whether that happened.

The next question is whether Treasury changes the program in ways that make those improvements more consistent.

In its latest quarterly plan, Treasury said it anticipates up to $70 billion in liquidity-support buybacks from November through January, alongside a separate $25 billion for cash management purposes. It also confirmed that it's evaluating changes to purchase limits, scheduling, frequency, eligibility, and execution.

That gives dealers a chance to see whether their feedback leads to something concrete.

The real market test won't be whether Treasury manages to spend its full buyback allowance. It'll be whether more bonds become easier to trade, dealers become more willing to handle customer orders, and financing pressures actually ease.

A $6 billion buyback might look impressive on paper, but for a dealer still holding a bond nobody wants to buy, the announcement's size only goes so far.

'We were quite lazy' - Fabian Hurzeler gives brutal Brighton verdict after narrow win in Getafe

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Malick Yalcouye fired Premier League high-flyers Brighton to a 1-0 win at Getafe but head coach Fabian Hurzeler was not pleased with the performance.

The impressive Yalcouye scored his fourth goal of the season to break the deadlock in a hard-fought encounter.

Did you know with an ad-lite subscription to SussexWorld, you get 70% fewer ads while viewing the news that matters to you.

The Seagulls are now the only team not to concede a goal in the Conference League so far and their seven points from three matches leaves them fifth in the table.

Brighton’s win in Madrid concludes a gruelling run of five consecutive away matches where they travelled to Jablonec, Liverpool, Manchester City (twice) and Getafe.

Hurzeler said his team deserved to win but also said they were lazy at times.

“That's what we expected,” said Hurzeler to TNT Sports after the match. “These games in the Conference League are different. You have to beat the low block, you have to understand what the opponents are looking for in the game and therefore you have to always be patient in these games and I think we did it today.

“We didn't play our best game, to be fair, we had a few moments where we were quite lazy with the easy things, we didn't do the simple things on the highest level, but in the end, you said it, we kept a clean sheet.

Hurzeler added: “The third clean sheet in a row in this tournament and then we scored in the right moment the goal and I think we deserve to win this game.”

Boss Fabian Hurzeler made five changes to the side which beat Manchester City at the weekend with Yalcouye joining Jaouen Hadjam, Michael Svobada, Jack Hinshelwood and Georginio Rutter in the starting line-up.

With the stadium undergoing renovation only 570 Brighton fans could attend, squeezed into a small corner of the north stand behind one of the goals.

They had a good view of Hinshelwood taking the first shot of the match, a 25-yard drive tipped wide by Getafe keeper David Soria.

Rutter then rolled defender Sebastian Boselli but skewed his shot across goal and wide.

They threatened again when Hadjam got in behind, only to lose his footing when he tried to cut back inside, with Rutter unable to convert the loose ball.

Brighton were almost caught out from a Getafe corner on the half-hour mark with Bart Verbruggen forced into a sharp reaction save from Ivan Azon’s snap-shot.

The Seagulls should have scored when Rutter’s through-ball sent Hadjam racing clean through. Hadjam got to the ball before Soria but his finish clipped the keeper and came back off a post, with Yalcouye’s attempt from the rebound blocked on the line.

Azon threatened the Brighton goal again in first-half stoppage time but was once again denied by Verbruggen.

After the break some good build-up play from Rutter and Hadjam set up substitute Luka Vuskovic, but his shot was always curling away from the target.

The breakthrough arrived in the 64th minute when Pascal Gross lifted a diagonal ball towards the far post.

Overlapping full-back Costinha had acres of space on the right of the penalty area and cushioned his header back across goal.

Yalcouye’s timing was impeccable as he met the ball with a thumping header which flew past Soria and in off the crossbar.

Ibrahim Osman could have doubled the lead seconds after coming on but he could not make contact with Diego Gomez’s low cross.

As Chargers' offense struggles, Mike McDaniel will move from the sideline to the booth

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Chargers offensive coordinator Mike McDaniel will get a different perspective on his struggling offense on Sunday against the Texans.

McDaniel said today that he will move upstairs to the booth to call plays after previously working from the sideline.

“One of the things that I want to try is call plays from the box to see adjustments for myself and communicate with the position coaches appropriately what things we can focus on between series,” McDaniel said. “I won’t be in the same spot.”

Although the Chargers’ offense has been among the worst in the NFL this season and head coach Jim Harbaugh has faced questions about whether he should fire his offensive coordinator, McDaniel insisted that he’s not concerned about the criticism.

“My problem solving doesn’t come from insecurity,” McDaniel said. “I’m not trying to prove or justify anything.”

The Chargers rank dead last in the NFL in scoring, having scored only 99 points this season, and they have an NFL-high 17 turnovers. McDaniel said he thinks it’s worth trying something different while delegating some of what he had been doing on the sideline to the assistant coaches.

“Seems like a good action to take based on how things have gone this year, and realistically something that I’ve only brought up because I’ve observed, I’m very confident in the rest of the offensive coaching staff and how they’re able to set the tone and communicate information on the sidelines. So I feel a little more comfortable to try that,” McDaniel said.

If that doesn’t work, Harbaugh’s next step might be to move McDaniel from the booth to the unemployment line.

T-Mobile owner on Elon Musk launching a wireless carrier: &#8216;Welcome to the party&#8217;

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Starlink Mobile could cost SpaceX at least $200 billion, Deutsche Telekom CEO Tim Höttges says.

Starlink Mobile could cost SpaceX at least $200 billion, Deutsche Telekom CEO Tim Höttges says.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The head of T-Mobile’s parent company is casting doubt on SpaceX’s plans to make Starlink Mobile a “major” carrier in the US. During an earnings call on Thursday, Deutsche Telekom CEO Tim Höttges says he takes the Elon Musk-owned company “super serious,” but that it will cost at least $200 billion to pull off:

He [Musk] has now bought 76 megahertz of spectrum. By the way, we have 400. So if you now say he can buy the spectrum prospectively, do your math. These are kind of triple-digit billions which are needed to gain the spectrum position. If he has the money, go for it. Then he has to build a network. I can tell you, we have 85,000 towers. No, he can rent towers, no question. But this is another $20 to $30 billion which are required.

Put antennas on it. You can buy antennas… and you can get [them] maybe even in a cheaper way, or you construct it in a new way, but you need money for that. So therefore, my assessment is that SpaceX at least — if they want to play in that industry — needs at least minimum $200 billion. But even beyond that, to play in an industry which we have, let’s say, professionalized over the last 20 years — okay, welcome to the party.

Last month, SpaceX laid out its plans for Starlink Mobile to compete against T-Mobile, AT&T, and Verizon by using a combination of direct-to-cell satellites and a terrestrial network. One recent report suggests that SpaceX could tap into 80,000 cell towers throughout the US, while another indicates that Musk was in talks to acquire Boost Mobile. This week, Qualcomm announced that SpaceX will use its cellular infrastructure technology to support Starlink Mobile’s network buildout.

At the same time, Höttges is aware of the potential threat Starlink Mobile could pose to the mobile industry by creating a “new” type of service.

“He [Musk] has chipsets, he has data centers, he has AI, he has satellites… He will go as well for maybe integrating social media or at least agent models into his service. So he is creating a new way of communication services,” Höttges says. “That is why I’m saying I take that super serious because I was witnessing what happened to the car industry. They were ignoring it for too long, and we have to face the situation. But it’s not a free lunch. And you see that with Tesla as well in the car industry.”

Back surgery will keep Robert Kraft from Rob Gronkowski's Patriots Hall of Fame induction

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Patriots owner Robert Kraft recently underwent back surgery, which will keep him sidelined on “injured reserve” and force him to miss Rob Gronkowski’s induction into the Patriots Hall of Fame on Saturday.

“As much pain as I was in before having surgery, it pains me even more to miss the ceremony,” Kraft, 85, told Mike Reiss of ESPN on Thursday.

Doctors ordered Kraft to stay off his feet, so he will also miss the Patriots’ game against the Packers on Sunday and the trip to Munich for a Week 10 game against the Lions.

Fans voted Gronkowski into the Patriots Hall of Fame in April in his first year of eligibility.

Gronkowski, having undergone multiple back surgeries during his playing career, understood Kraft’s absence, the owner said.

“I always told Gronk that in my next life, I wanted to come back as him, because I had never seen him have a bad day. But after a couple of back surgeries, I take that back. I never want to experience that again,” Kraft said.

New West Records Names Brad Turcotte as General Manager

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The label's artist roster includes American Aquarium and Amos Lee.

New West Records has named Brad Turcotte as general manager. The label’s artist roster includes Billy Joe Shaver, Amos Lee, American Aquarium, 49 Winchester and All Them Witches.

Most recently, Turcotte has served in roles including artist manager at Vector Management, senior vp at Australia-based live entertainment promoter TEG Live and partner at marketing agency 615 Leverage + Strategy.

Turcotte has also previously held leadership roles at major labels including UMG Nashville (now MCA) and Big Machine Label Group. Along the way, he’s worked with artists including Taylor Swift, Chris Stapleton, Keith Urban, Luke Bryan and Carrie Underwood. The move to New West Records marks a return to Turcotte’s indie label roots, which began with his founding of Houston-based Compadre Records in 2001.

“This year has brought a few unexpected detours for me professionally, but I truly feel like the road has ended at the best home possible,” Turcotte said in a statement. “I can’t thank John Allen and George Fontaine Sr. enough for trusting me with this role. Many moons ago, when I opened Compadre Records in Houston, one of our main competitors was another young independent label just down the road in Austin called New West Records. We were both trying to build something meaningful, champion great artists and make our mark on the music business. To now have the opportunity to help lead New West feels like an incredible full-circle moment.”

Turcotte also serves on the board of directors for the nonprofit music education program Music Will, which aids music education in the U.S. public school system.

Taiwan’s Content Agency Chief Sue Wang Puts IP Commercialization at Center of Strategy

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Sue Wang, chair of the Taiwan Creative Content Agency (TAICCA), says Taiwan’s content industry should make IP development and commercialization its top priority.

“The challenge is not simply whether we can create good content, but whether companies have the capabilities and business models to turn creative ideas into sustainable revenue, build scalable businesses, and reach audiences across different markets,” Wang says as the 2026 Taiwan Creative Content Fest (TCCF), which runs Nov. 6-9 in Taipei, gets underway. “Without a viable business model that has been tested in the market, simply introducing more funding or investment programs will not necessarily create a sustainable growth cycle.” Related Stories 'Love Language' Review: Chloë Grace Moretz Sells Words of Affirmation in an Old-School Romcom With Gen-Z Trappings Despite Hudson Williams, Canadian Witch Mystery 'Yaga' Is No 'Heated Rivalry': TV Review

Wang says that without IP with demonstrated market potential, investment risks stay high, and even overseas distribution may not turn into sustained sales. TAICCA wants to help businesses strengthen their pricing, licensing, branding and audience-development capabilities first, then bring in growth capital and international distribution.

As of Oct. 30, TAICCA had invested about NT$560 million ($17.1 million) in 24 international co-financing and co-production projects across feature films, series and animation, with partners in 15 countries and territories. One example is Taiwan-Japan film “Arrested Memory,” selected for Venice Open – Out of Competition at the 83rd Venice Film Festival and the Midnight Madness section of the 51st Toronto Film Festival.

TCCF’s Market will bring together 107 exhibitors from seven countries and territories. Exhibitors in IP categories such as publishing, comics and characters have increased 13% from last year.

The first Spotlight Screenings will present “April” on Nov. 7 and Taiwan-Singapore series “See You at 11pm” on Nov. 8. Wang says the goal is to raise the international profile of Taiwanese actors and give buyers a chance to meet the actors and creative teams behind the titles.

This year’s Forum, themed “Reshape,” will feature 12 talks and panels with more than 20 experts on topics including international co-production, streaming consolidation, IP, short-form content and AI. TCCF’s Pitching, Market and Forum sections connect projects with development support, investors, production resources and buyers. Selected Pitching teams may also apply for early-stage support through TAICCA’s Cultural Content Development Program.

Wang says TAICCA wants to deepen ties with France in Cultural Tech, building on more than a decade of cooperation. The Taiwan-France Cultural Award will mark its 30th anniversary in 2027. With South Korea, the agency hopes to expand collaboration beyond screen content into independent music, live venues, festivals and the performing arts.

The Taiwan-Korea Entertainment and Cultural Content Fund and the Far EasTone Creative Entertainment Fund’s Enjoy Entertainment Limited Partnership have approved investments in 12 projects, mobilizing about NT$600 million ($18.3 million) in private capital, Wang says. Projects include the Kaohsiung Park Music Festival, developed with CJ ENM’s Seoul Park Music Festival; the 2026 ID. Music Festival, in collaboration with Spotify; and the concert IP “Her Voice: The Fearless Female Voice.” On the film side, the funds have backed road movie “Lonely Together” and “The Rope Curse 4: Kuntilanak,” the latest installment in Taiwan’s “The Rope Curse” horror franchise.

TAICCA sees AI mainly as a supporting tool for technical work such as animation, visual effects and post-production. Wang says the agency is cautious about generative AI in core creative work, including scriptwriting and music composition, citing uncertain public acceptance and the amount of human correction AI-generated material can require.

“There is no established playbook or ready-made solution for navigating the changes currently taking place in the industry,” Wang says. “We don’t want to see a situation where people end up working as assistants to AI.”

Over the next three years, Wang hopes to see several medium-sized or larger production companies emerge, each producing more than 10 titles a year. She also wants international co-production to become a regular practice and more private and technology-sector investment to flow into Taiwan’s content industry.

America's Real 1% Has Nothing to Do With Silicon Valley or Wall Street

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The wealthiest people in America aren't who you think they are. Together, they have more net worth than the Forbes 400 by a factor of 10 — and almost no one has heard of them.

In this episode of Motley Fool Hidden Gems Investing, Motley Fool contributor Rachel Warren talks with Eric Zwick, professor of economics at the University of Chicago Booth School of Business and co-author of The Everywhere Millionaire, about the decade of research that rewrote what he thought he knew about wealth, inequality, and the American economy.They discuss:

To catch full episodes of all The Motley Fool's free podcasts, check out our podcast center. When you're ready to invest, check out this top 10 list of stocks to buy.

This podcast was recorded on Oct. 25, 2026.

Eric Zwick: When you collectively add them up, they have more net worth than the Forbes 400 by an order of magnitude, so more than 10 times the collective net worth of the Forbes 400, even though individually, they're not as rich as Elon.

Rachel Warren: That was Eric Zwick, Professor of Economics at the University of Chicago Booth School of Business. On a group of Americans almost no one has ever heard of who collectively hold more wealth than the entire Forbes 400. I'm Motley Fool analyst Rachel Warren. Eric spent a decade working through economic data that most researchers never get access to, and what he found completely rewrites the story of wealth in this country. We get into who actually makes up the real 1%, what this hidden economy of Main Street millionaires means for investors watching the public markets, and the statistic that made Eric rethink everything he thought he knew about inequality. We hope you enjoy.

Welcome back to Motley Fool Conversations. I'm Motley Fool analyst Rachel Warren. Today, I'm delighted to welcome Eric Zwick, Professor of Economics and Finance at the University of Chicago Booth School of Business to the show. Eric is also the co-author of an incredibly eye-opening new book, The Everywhere Millionaire. Who is really rich in America and how they got there? Eric has spent a decade digging through economic data to shatter the myth that transformational wealth belongs only to Silicon Valley tech founders and Wall Street executives. Instead, this research has uncovered America's hidden 1%, the regional business owners, pass-through entity founders, and Main Street entrepreneurs, building generational fortunes completely away from the spotlight. Eric, it's wonderful to have you on the show. Welcome.

Eric Zwick: Thank you so much for having me. Great to be here.

Rachel Warren: One of the things that struck me is the core thesis of The Everywhere Millionaire challenges the popular narrative that massive wealth creation requires building a software giant or managing a hedge fund, and there are many other examples. When you look at the data, what does the typical member of America's real 1% actually look like?

Eric Zwick: Yes, so we're told that the path to prosperity is through Silicon Valley or Wall Street, you have to be a tech CEO or a VC-backed start-up founder or a Wall Street trader in order to get really rich in America. What we found through a decade of research, working with unprecedented administrative data that then in the book, we turned into stories by going out and finding these Everywhere Millionaires lurking in plain sight. Wealth is much more pervasive. It's much more abundant across the country. There are people running unglamorous businesses in towns and suburbs and Tier 2, Tier 3 cities all across the country, who are incredibly wealthy. Our average millionaires have an average net worth of around $25 million. They have minimum net worth of about $5 million. They are people who own a private business, and we estimate there are about three million of them.

That's a lot of people. When you collectively add them up, they have more net worth than the Forbes 400 by an order of magnitude. More than 10 times the collective net worth of the Forbes 400, even though individually, they're not as rich as Elon. They're just collectively this huge group of people that we really didn't know that much about, I think, for various reasons that we can talk about. Concretely, who are they? We do something we joke because we both have little kids, on and I, the ABC board book. The ABCs of getting rich. A is for auto dealers, B is for beer distributors, C is for contractors. D is for dentists and it could go on. But it's like that type of mid market business owner across a whole bunch of industries that are not super glamorous. These are the Everywhere Millionaires whose stories we try and tell in this book.

Rachel Warren: I think in the book as well, you call this group the stealthy, wealthy. I wonder why has this segment of the population managed to build this wealth, but very much away from the spotlight and even the radar of Wall Street?

Eric Zwick: We call them stealthy, wealthy, and some are maybe nefarious a little bit in their behavior, but many, they're just flying under the radar, but just not with anything untoward about their behavior. We call them the stealthy wealthy just cause people aren't looking for them in the right places. Looking away from the coasts. A lot of our attention is drawn to the coasts, where we tend to look in the media at public company activity. A lot of retail investors listen to a podcast. Public companies are specific subset of the American economy, and they disclose their information. You know what they are. They tend to be consumer-oriented businesses, not always. They tend to be in more capital-intensive sectors, so they have to raise capital to go public. This is not a representative pick of the American economy, and it's not the ballpark in terms of where people work, where the profits are.

More than half of the total profits in business across the country are in what we call pass-through businesses. These are non-public businesses. There's a ton of activity. Most businesses in the country are these pass-through businesses, non-public businesses, and they’re all over the place. I think the Forbes 400 is another example of when people think about who's really rich, they jump to the top of the Forbes list. Those are tech founders, typically public companies owners, and also people who are seeking attention in some cases by buying media companies. Our stealthy, wealthy are not actively trying to hide, although some are. One car dealer is notoriously referred to as Batman because everybody knows that he exists, but nobody's seen him. I think they just are running their businesses. They're not in these media markets, and they're doing their thing and enjoying it in a lot of cases, without a lot of attention at all.

Rachel Warren: One of the things that struck me I saw there was a comment on your book from the Nobel Prize-winning economist Angus Deaton, and he was saying that in his comment, there's currently five million households in America with over $5 million each running entirely below the radar. I wonder, what does that tell us about just the scaling capacity, but also the overall health of the U.S. consumer economy?

Eric Zwick: I think there's this fisheye lens view of the U.S. economy that is prevailing in the media narrative where it zooms in on Elon or zooms in on a few billionaires. Then it's like everybody is being left behind. I think the message from these stories is that there's a lot more opportunity for people to do incredibly well. We're not talking about people who just have a million bucks in a pension. We're talking about people who don't really have to think too hard about how they spend their money at this point. They're close to post economic, if not post economic, to use a term of art, where they basically, I want this. I'm going to buy it. There are millions of people like that across the country, and many of them weren't born with a lot of opportunities.

We figure that about a third we try and estimate this in various ways of our Everywhere Millionaires inherited businesses or come through that shortcut into prosperity as business owners, but that means the two thirds or maybe 70% either founded their business or bought one and grew it later in their careers. That's a lot of self made stories in the data. We tell some of these stories individually, **** Portillo from Chicago, who started out growing up in the Cabrini Green Housing Project and started a hot dog stand. He didn't actually know how to make hot dogs at the time. He didn't know how to steam a bun. Had an $1,100 investment initially. Built this thing incrementally with no outside investors, very little debt, just plowing his profits back into the business. It was the largest privately owned restaurant company by the time he eventually sold it because he didn't really want to pass it on to his kids. He was business, and so he was I'm going to sell it in his 80s, although he was annoyed to do it after selling it, he's told us he was bored. But he sold it for over a billion dollars, and we found him in yacht registration data.

He has a very large yacht, a 100-foot yacht called Top Dog. There are stories like this. He's an outlier within that class because he's more like a billionaire that people haven't heard of. But there are just tons of stories that look like him in the data, and I think it's quite inspiring, and it says that there's more opportunity available than I think the prevailing narrative. I think it's an optimistic take on the economy that you don't hear as often as maybe we should.

Rachel Warren: What do you think maybe the primary misconceptions are among the public, among investors about where that real durable wealth is being generated right now?

Eric Zwick: Silicon Valley tech apps, influencer economy. These are terms that I think are the flashy Instagram reel images that we see about rich people. We don't see the trash bag liner manufacturer who supplies to all the McDonald's franchises. Then when he retired from that business, bought a submersible so he could hunt for actual treasure in the Caribbean from sunk in treasure ships. We don't see the guy who has 20 Wendy's franchises that he's built that portfolio of franchises over time running Wendy's, who's maybe a decca millionaire or something like that. These are unglamorous businesses. They don't draw our attention in the same way, even though the people are laughing their way to the bank.

They're not as entertaining to look at if you're swiping and scrolling. I think that's why there's just this misconception about it. But once you start to look for them, you do see them everywhere, even if you live in New York City, you actually can see them all over the place. I live in Chicago. I walk from work to school to drop off my kid. There's a beer truck that says Budweiser on the side, and on the side of the door is the name of the distributor. I'm — that's Reyes. That's one of the largest beer distributors in the country, incredibly wealthy, and most people haven't heard of him or that family, and their story is quite interesting in terms of how they rolled up distributors across the country over many decades. But there's that kind of thing. They're right all around us closer to home than you think. But you're not looking for them on your phone, you're looking for them in the world around you.

Rachel Warren: From your research into these Main Street businesses, I wonder what are some takeaways or a framework that maybe you can draw into evaluating public companies? If you're a retail investor listening to this conversation, what are maybe some lessons that can be learned from these private businesses that can be drawn into the health of the public markets?

Eric Zwick: That's a good question. I like that question. One is probably to think about competition a little bit more broadly. There's scope for competition from the private market in certain product lines. Thinking about when you're picking investments, it's not just about competition from other publics. It's competition from products that are being offered just by these Everywhere Millionaires and their businesses, and which ones are robust to that competition? Which ones have an opportunity to disrupt the sleepier Everywhere Millionaires in certain industries.

I think we've seen legal services, companies that are trying to enter and disrupt, say, these law practices across the country because there's tons of profit there. If you're looking for opportunities or growth, I think disrupting some of these businesses where they're not so focused on staying ahead or adopting new technology. I think it's quite interesting. We've seen a little bit of that with the software as a service. Is this going to be disrupted by AI or is it going to be complimentary with AI? I think we could think about some of these more tactile industries, fast casual dining or auto parts, or mid market manufacturing, making frozen quiches, like this stuff in big box stores.

Is AI going to disrupt all aspects of that business or is AI going to help with customer relations management is going to help with sales? What does that mean for some of the other stocks I'm looking at or some of the opportunities I'm looking at? I think it's useful to have a reminder that the economy isn't just zeros and ones and chips and code, that there's this much richer in the world three dimensional economy out there, and the stories might be a little different than the Magnificent 7. I think, as a public market investor, that's what we think about all the investors are flooding into those seven bets. What are some, maybe interesting contrarian bets? There's some thoughts I have on that question.

Rachel Warren: One of the other things that I was thinking about often, when we look at the public markets, we see some companies expend massive amounts of capital on dilutive acquisitions or even poorly time share buybacks. I wonder how do the stealthy, wealthy, as you call them in the book? How do they tend to think about capital allocation? Because I also think that there's often parallels that we can draw when we're looking at quality businesses in the public markets from what we see in terms of the actions taking place in the private markets.

Eric Zwick: I see some of these acquisitions or throwing capital back at investors through buybacks, it's the consequence of having run out of good ideas. In some cases the dilutive acquisition is, well, we see the writing on the wall with our main business line, which is trending down. In the absence of this acquisition, it's going to look even worse. It could still make sense to do it, even if it's not a great, it's not going to lead to salvation for the company. Stealthy, wealthy folks have the benefit of being able to grow without reinventing themselves in a lot of cases, as I mentioned, geographically or adding a few products like the chemical importer exporter that we profile can just find a few more customers can tell you hard to do, but because their market shares are so much smaller, it's an easier problem to solve to try and grow that business, at least conceptually. Sales is not easy if nobody's heard of you, so you really got to get in the door and go to the customers and talk to them and work that network for sure.

But they have a bit of an easier job growing, especially if they're in a growing industry, so then they can just ride those tailwinds. Big public company that's very established market leader. Dilutive acquisition might be the only game in town, or returning money to shareholders might be the only game in town. I think maybe the stealthy, wealthy version of that is, well, reaching the end of my life cycle going to retire. I don't know who's going to take over the business, so maybe I'll just sell this thing to private equity and ride off into the sunset on my yacht. That's what some of those stories look like. It's returning the money to shareholders, as opposed to burning the thing down to maintain control.

ADVERTISEMENT: Every Sunday, we cover the week's tech news on this Weekend Tech. Hi, this is Leo Laporte from the TWiT Podcast Network, inviting you to join me this week for a doozy. Jason Calacanis and Gina Smith join me. We're going to talk about so many things. The flying car from Tesla, the guy who bugged the OpenAI conference room, wrapped it in toilet paper, and the new Surface laptop -- it’s all coming up on TWiT. You'll find it at twit.tv and wherever you get your podcasts.

Rachel Warren: It's interesting to think about how as investors in public companies who gain indirect exposure to these players just by nature of their relationships with some of these big players. When you were writing this book, as we get to the end of our time here together today, I'm curious, What surprised you the most in your research as you were learning about these Main Street entrepreneurs and their businesses?

Eric Zwick: I came at this thinking about the stories on especially like top inequality was maybe one of the early things that we thought about. Piketty’s Capital had just come out, that book that was all about. We're back in the Gilded Age. It's a new Gilded Age. A few billionaire industrialists that are taking over the whole economy. These massive companies, and that's where all the money at the top is. We started to look at, well, what's driving the top 1% share in income? Which in Picketty's book, the top 1% is this nameless, faceless group of people. You don't really get a lot of humans. We joke about the book as Piketty with people, but it’s like you actually meet these people that are in the top 1% and see what they’re doing and get a little bit about their stories from their own mouths. You're, Oh, this is not the typical one percenter or even 0.1 percenter is not like the money bags monopolist, Gilded Age person. It's like a private business owner with a mid market business. There are tons of these people. The top 1%. When we looked at narrow industries, the industry generating the most profits for their owners was doctors' offices. I was, Oh, doctors' offices, that doesn't feel like a capital intensive business at all.

That's really just they are running practices, they've structured as businesses, so they can pay themself a bit lower wages, but higher profits get better tax treatment, but also scale the business up and sell some of the services and so on, that doctors do. But there's just a ton of doctors, and healthcare is 18% of the economy, and a big chunk of it is not hospitals or pharmaceuticals. It's just services being provided by these people. I was, Okay, this is a totally different view on inequality and who's really rich than I entered and you just trying to answer that question. That blew my mind.

The more we talk to people or tell them about what we're learning in the data, not just economists or academics, but just like friends, family, people that we encounter, conferences, and so on, people that I play golf with. They just know all these people. They know these stories. They recognize the stories pretty quickly from their own lives, from their own backgrounds, from where they grew up, from people they know, their regional banker, from who they're lending to, the magnet manufacturer, this kind of thing. I think you realize they're all around us and we just haven't really been looking for them in the right way. It was very eye-opening to me, and I think a lot of people who have read the book, which was written not just to be collected, but also to be enjoyed and read, will have that eye-opening experience, I believe.

Rachel Warren: One final question for you as well. Just as investors who are hearing all of this and drawing on the lessons that you've showed us today, I also wonder, what are some key elements, metrics we can be looking at tracking the health of the economy, tracking the health markets. If I'm an investor listening, trying to see how can I be tracking the health of the economy and the markets over the next 12-18 months? What would you be looking at?

Eric Zwick: That's good. Thinking about these Everywhere Millionaires, some of the more aggregate, tangible retail sales, consumption data. These businesses are often selling, say, the fast casual dining. You're looking at those kinds of metrics. That's not just quarterly earnings, but really what's going on with consumption? Are people able to spend money or people pulling back because prices have gone up? I think that's an important thing going forward on the economy and that's where through a lot of these types of businesses, they'll grow if customers keep coming in the door and they'll stop growing. They won't close, but they'll stop growing if traffic slows down. Then if you're in the supply chain, there are a lot of cost shocks. We're looking at some of the PPI numbers to think about where are inputs, especially tight.

What are some of the second and third order effects of, say, energy prices going up? Well, fertilizer goes into food, so food costs, or you have all the distribution logistics and trucking and so on, that uses fuel. Well, that means like everything that moves around that needs to go from warehouse to consumer, warehouse to store. That's going to be more expensive. You track some of that stuff. A lot of them Main Street millionaires are running the businesses along that supply chain or in those input providing. You can see in some of the aggregate data that comes out of the statistical agencies, you can see the health of the economy that way. I think those are some of the problems that we face today. I'm looking at some of those statistics to see where things are going in the next 12-18.

Rachel Warren: Thank you so much for that, Eric. It's been so great to speak with you today, and for those who are listening or watching, check out Eric's new book co-authored with Owen Zidar, The Everywhere Millionaire: Who Is Really Rich In America and How They Got There. Thank you so much, Eric.

Matt Damon Reveals Why He Passed on Starring in ‘Ted’

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

'The Odyssey' star would've played John Bennett, a role that went to fellow Boston native Mark Wahlberg.

In a new interview with GQ, Damon revealed that he passed on starring in Seth MacFarlane‘s 2012 comedy as John Bennett, the best friend of the foul-mouthed, party-loving teddy bear named Ted.

“I don’t want to live with the regret, but I can tell you that I passed on Ted, and I’ve never had this experience where I laughed so hard reading a script,” The Odyssey star said. “I literally took it to dinner and was reading parts of it out to my friends at dinner. And then I just got too clever by half. I don’t know what I was thinking. I was like, Well, maybe the teddy bear’s actually the best part.…” Related Stories Movies Christopher Nolan's 'The Odyssey' Will Return to Theaters Next Month as Awards Season Heats Up Lifestyle GQ Expands Men of the Year Footprint in L.A.

Damon went on to explain why he decided to go in a different direction — and why he still thinks about what could have been.

“I overthought it and I talked myself out of it,” he said. “And because it shot in Boston, it would’ve been a chance for me to be home — my father’s passed away now, and I look back and I go, Man, that would’ve been six months back [home].”

The role ultimately went to fellow Boston native Mark Wahlberg, who stars alongside the talking teddy bear in the 2015 sequel, Ted 2.

Damon praised Wahlberg’s performance, saying the “right actor” got the part and that the movie turned out great. “I wouldn’t have made it any better,” he added.

The success of the two films later spawned a Ted prequel series on Peacock, which ran for two seasons.

Speaking with The Hollywood Reporter earlier this year, the Family Guy creator discussed the franchise’s future after the series ended and said he would be interested in making a third film.

“It’s up to Universal. It’s up to Peacock. A third season in the near future — I don’t know that there are any plans for that,” he said. “I’ve thrown the idea out that maybe we do a direct-to-Peacock Ted feature with this cast. That might be something that’s kind of fun. It was what, 10 years between the last Ted movie and the first episode of this series, and people were still there for it. He’s a character we can bring back at any time.”

What Tom Gardner Said About Days Like This

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Tom didn't predict today's drop. He did explain why it shouldn't surprise us and what Fools should do about it.

If you opened your portfolio today and winced, you're in good company. As of market close, the S&P 500 was down 4.36% and the Nasdaq 5.52%. If you own a lot of growth stocks, your account probably fell harder than that.

Back in September, Tom Gardner sat down to record his sixth Quarterly Call, and he spent a good chunk of it on days like this one. He didn't predict today. Tom doesn't make market calls, and he gave "a mild wrist slap" to people who make spectacular ones. Instead, he explained why a drop like this shouldn't catch a Fool off guard and what to do when one arrives. Here are five ideas from that call worth rereading before you touch the sell button.

Tom reminded us that the S&P 500 has historically fallen 10% about once a year. A pullback of 10% to 15%, he said, is "par for the course." Think of it as the toll you pay for decades of stock market returns.

He also argued the odds of a pullback were higher than usual when he recorded the call. The S&P 500 and Nasdaq were trading 10% to 30% above their typical historical valuation ranges. When stocks are priced for good news, it takes less bad news to knock them down.

And he warned that when the index falls, the high-growth, high-beta stocks many Fools own tend to fall further. High beta means a stock swings harder than the market in both directions. So if your portfolio is down more than the S&P 500 today, that's the trade-off Tom told us to expect.

"Emotional reactivity is the enemy of wealth creation," Tom said. A red day tempts you toward the dramatic move: Sell everything, or back up the truck.

Tom's alternative is incrementalism. Buy and sell in stages and recheck your thesis at every step. His example came from Hidden Gems, which sold 15% of its CrowdStrike (CRWD -9.88%) position in the weeks before the call. Tom said the team didn't expect calamity for the business. The stock looked richly priced, so they took some cash off the table and kept the rest.

Tom argued that moving in steps builds conviction. Each partial sale makes you rethink your reasons, and that can get you out of a bad position sooner than an all-or-nothing standoff in your head. If you feel the urge to do something today, do a small something.

Tom's first recommendation for this market was to pair higher-quality companies with lower return expectations. He used Costco (COST +1.83%) to show what he means.

He thinks Costco could deliver about 10% a year plus a small dividend. Over a decade, that turns $10,000 into roughly $27,000. Tom admitted that sounds dull to anyone who's seen a stock do 2.7 times its money in six months. So he asked members to picture Costco handing them about 10% a year for the next decade and to decide honestly whether that would satisfy them. Your answer, he said, sets the frame for how you invest from here.

The danger runs the other way. Lower-quality companies carrying high expectations can fall 50%, 70%, or 90% "and never come back," Tom warned. If one of those becomes one of your largest positions, it can be "quite damaging to your pursuit of financial freedom," he said, noting that it's a good idea to have "solid ballast" in your portfolio. Ballast is the heavy weight in a ship's hold that keeps it steady.

Tom reaffirmed a core Foolish principle: Own 50 or more companies and hold them for more than five years on average.

His reminder of why came from a stock he owns. Rocket Lab (RKLB -12.18%) had run up to $150 a share and then been cut in half by the time of the call. Tom still believes in the business, but he called that kind of swing a reminder of "the importance of having a diversified portfolio."

Tom's final action step was about your household, and it may matter most on a day like today. He suggested a family exercise. Figure out how cheaply you could live for a month and still enjoy your life. Then ask how big your emergency fund should be. Is six months enough? Nine? Twelve?

His goal is to simulate a recession, a market decline, or a job change before any of them shows up. "Taking the step to improve the sustainability of a high-quality life is vastly more significant and important than squeezing out extra percentage points of returns, particularly in a richly priced market," he said.

A household with its bills covered and cash in reserve can treat a falling market as a sale. Tom described investors who get through a 20% or even 40% decline "in style." They keep a cash position on the side and put it to work in their favorite companies at lower prices. In a reply to members in the call's comments section, Tom said his own cash position sits in the 10% to 15% range.

Tom closed the first section of his call with a line from his father, who created the Potential Growth Indicator you'll find on our site. After a long, happy day, when the Gardner kids asked to stay up late, their dad would say, "Tomorrow's another day." Tom offered that advice to investors who try to cram every win into today. It works just as well for losses. A bad day is one day, and Fools invest for decades.

Tom Gardner has positions in CrowdStrike and Rocket Lab. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Costco Wholesale, CrowdStrike, and Rocket Lab. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

‘SNL’ Writer Jimmy Fowlie Pays Tribute to Sister Christina Downer After Police Arrest Two for Her Murder

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

"It brings our family and her friends some relief to know that charges have been filed in connection with Christina’s death... Today I want to spend a little time remembering how much fun I had with her and how lucky I am that she was my sister," Fowlie wrote in a post for Downer's birthday.

Saturday Night Live writer Jimmy Fowlie is paying tribute to his sister, Christina Downer, on her birthday almost a week after police announced they have arrested two people for her murder.

Fowlie shared an Instagram post of himself and Downer at a movie theater in front of a promotional photo-op for Tron: Ares, which he noted was a “video of what turned out to be our last moment together.” He also thanked “everyone who’s reached out” and addressed news that Downer’s boyfriend, Delorean Bijon Brandon, and Kaylee Nicole Markowski had been charged with her murder. Related Stories TV 'SNL': Inde Navarrette Digs at Shane Gillis' Joke About Women Dressing Like "Whores" at the Gym as Tom Hanks Crashes Cold Open News LAPD Arrests Two for Murder of Christina Downer, Sister to 'SNL' Writer Jimmy Fowlie

“It brings our family and her friends some relief to know that charges have been filed in connection with Christina’s death. Because this whole experience has been so endlessly depressing, today I want to spend a little time remembering how much fun I had with her and how lucky I am that she was my sister,” he wrote at the end of the post. “Happy birthday, Chris!”

The Los Angeles Police Department announced on Oct. 30 that Brandon and Markowski had been arrested exactly on Oct. 29. “On December 18, 2025, Christina Downer was reported missing after her ex-husband requested a welfare check when he had not heard from her for an extended period of time. Downer’s boyfriend, Delorean Brandon, claimed that she had moved out several months earlier, leaving behind all her belongings, including her vehicle and dog,” the LAPD said in a statement.

In their statement, police said, “Robbery-Homicide Division detectives assumed the investigation after Downer’s cellphone was discovered washed ashore on Santa Monica Beach. Through various investigative techniques, detectives identified Delorean Brandon and Kaylee Markowski as the individuals responsible for Downer’s murder, the disposal of her body, and the concealment of their involvement.”

Elswhere in Fowlie’s tribute, he described the events that led up to he and Downer’s night at the movies captured in his Instagram video.

“It truly was just an inconsequential night at the movies. I’ve always thought those little photo ops at the Grove were so puzzling. I’d see them and try to imagine a world where me and my friends would pose in front of one non-ironically. For some reason on this day, i decided to make @nletreize take one of me and Christina as we were leaving,” he wrote. I just wanted to be extra and make her laugh, and I love how you can see her giggling as she walks up. She’s wearing her slippers which i think is so cute. In this video i feel like you can feel how sweet her energy is.”

The SNL writer continued, “She was one of my favorite people to make laugh. You could argue my whole comedy career was actually born out of sitting at the dinner table as kids and acting dumb while our dad became increasingly irate at me. It was a sacrifice that was always worth it.”

On Dec. 22, 2025, Fowlie shared a social media post asking for help finding his missing sister, sharing a multiple of images of Downer. Months later, in April of this year, he announced that “the LAPD has informed our family that Christina is no longer alive, and the case has officially transitioned from a missing person to a homicide investigation.”

The LAPD’s Robbery-Homicide Division will present the case to the L.A. Country District Attorney’s Office for filing consideration. The investigation is still ongoing.

4 Speed Records and Free Solos That Went Down in the Last 2 Weeks

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Becca Droz (left) and Kate Kelleghan (right) set a new female speed record on the classic 'Naked Edge' in Boulder's Eldorado Canyon on October 26. (Photo: COROS)

Maybe it’s the erratic weather pressures of El Niño, or perhaps there’s just a particularly competitive spirit in the air right now. In the past two weeks, several climbers have broken records, made improbable repeats, and soloed terrain that would make many of us squirm.

Last week, we looked into the culture of free solo speed climbing in Boulder, Colorado, speaking with several athletes about the risks and influences they carry in their high-stakes sport. On the heels of this deep dive, two of the athletes who spoke to us went on to break additional records of their own within the past few days. This includes a new FKT on Half Dome and a fresh women’s speed record on Eldorado’s most famous route.

More broadly, we’ve seen a few historic achievements in the past couple weeks by free soloists and speed athletes alike. Here are four record-breakers we’ve been watching lately, in chronological order.

Just a few weeks ago, Alex Honnold told Adrian Ballinger that he thought someone would likely repeat his free solo of El Capitan before anyone dared to repeat his insanely hard linkup of peaks and scrambles in Red Rock. Back in 2022, he dubbed that linkup the Honnold Ultimate Red Rock Traverse (aka the HURT).

Honnold will have to eat his words, because on October 19, Nevada climbing guide Nik Mirhashemi put down the world’s second HURT, with slight modifications. Friends have been calling his revised route the NURT: Nik’s Ultimate Red Rock Traverse.

For the NURT, the 36-year-old skipped four non-technical summits, including Goodman, Buffalo Wall, Sandstone North, and Limestone North. Instead he traversed from White Rock Peak to ascend the seven-pitch Frigid Air Buttress (5.9+; 800ft). He then continued to the top of Icebox Peak. “Basically, I had to choose between adding Frigid Air or sticking with the OG route, where those nontechnical peaks are,” Mirhashemi said. “I didn’t want to skip Frigid Air as it is quite classic.” He also called the section he swapped in one of the traverse’s “routefinding cruxes.”

Despite the variations, Mirhashemi doesn’t think there’s a significant difference in effort level. He said that though his traverse is slightly shorter, he added more technical terrain, making the difference essentially a wash. “Nice to add a little personal flair to these things I suppose,” Mirhashemi said.

“I wanted to try the HURT for similar reasons to Alex,” Mirhashemi told Climbing. “Super long days of soloing moderate terrain is one of my favorite things to do. I’ve been coming out to Red Rock for many years and just moved here a year ago. I’m always looking for new ways to challenge myself and it just made sense as a goal right in my backyard.”

Last spring, Mirhashemi made his first HURT attempt, then completed several “scoping missions”  to prepare for his fall go. “I was never sure if I could pull it off,” he said. “Stoked it’s not haunting me anymore!”

Kate Kelleghan has been busy breaking her own record again, shaving minutes and seconds off the female fastest known time on The Naked Edge (5.11b), a classic multi-pitch in Colorado’s Eldorado Canyon.

Kelleghan, along with her French partner Laura Pineau, already held the record for the route’s women’s speed record of 37 minutes and eight seconds, which was 32 seconds faster than Kelleghan’s previous go in 2021 with Becca Droz, a Boulder-based climbing guide.

In late October, Kelleghan began working the route again with Droz. And on October 26, the athletes set a new record of 36 minutes, 26 seconds, shaving 42 seconds off the previous record.

“Becca and I were the team that started the women’s record, so taking it back together feels full circle for our friendship and our community,” Kelleghan said. Droz added that being able to track splits, as well as time out the approach, actual climb, and descent with their COROS watches “was a game changer”; both women are COROS athletes.

The men’s record is held by John Ebers and Ben Wilbur at 24 minutes and 14 seconds.

Just a couple months after smashing the speed record on the First Flatiron in Boulder, 32-year-old American climber Michael Reese established a fastest known time on Yosemite’s Half Dome on October 28. In so doing, he broke an 11-year FKT set by the late Dean Potter. Reese climbed the 2,000-foot batholith from the Mist Trailhead to its summit in 1 hour, 12 minutes, and 58 seconds. He followed Potter’s original line up Grizzly Gully, then free soloed Snake Dike (5.7 R; 800ft). He broke Potter’s previous record set on May 3, 2015 by about six minutes.

A few days after his Half Dome lap, our reporter Holly Chen, who also reported on his Flatiron speed record, caught up with Reese.

“In 2021, one of Dean Potter’s good friends told me about this as a potential record to try. He became one of my main climbing partners,” Reese told Chen. Since then, Reese has made 16 attempts and several scouting missions before his record-breaking attempt in late October.

“Records are made to be broken. I’m not gonna be surprised if it’s broken one day,” Reese said.

In the second-hardest free solo by a woman, Aleksandra “Ola” Taistra climbed a seven-pitch, 720-foot route in Sardinia, Italy on October 29. The line in question is Margarites (7a+/5.12a) up the limestone cliffs of Monte Oddeu. Established in 1998 by Andrea Calvo and Lorenzo Nadali, the route has become one of Sardinia’s “most sought-after lines,” according to Taistra. It includes a bouldery 7a+/5.12a crux pitch high off the deck; two 7a/5.11d pitches; and four additional pitches graded 6c+/5.11c, 6b+/5.11a, 6a+/5.10c, and 5c/5.10a, in order of descending difficulty.

“Climbing Margarites for the first time I thought that climbing it without a rope was impossible for me,” Taistra shared with Climbing, “and that’s exactly how the plan was born—first get to know the route, and then properly assess my skills.” Taistra says that when she finished free soloing Margarites, she thought: “Oh, that’s it?”

Taistra already put down one of the hardest female free solos in history in May, when she climbed Alfredo Alfredo (5.11d/7a) ropeless. Now, the only known harder female free solo belongs to French legend Catherine Destivelle, who climbed an unnamed route in Mali without a rope that she said maxed out at 7b or 5.12a/b.

In May, 44-year-old Taistra told Climbing that free soloing gives her “so much peace” and feels less stressful than climbing with a rope. The 44-year-old climber rehearses her routes on toprope until she feels confident in the moves.
